<details><summary>Notes and gotchas (31)</summary>

- **Account Access screen (Settings → Account Access tab)** — Client-only screen, but it lives on the working branch 'azure-phase-5-8-server' which is NOT deployed. Verified today: `git show main:index.html \| grep -c editAccessPolicy` = 0. Nobody can reach this screen on the live site.
- **The permission grid (20 jobs × 6 account types)** — The grid is data-driven — if a policy ever contained a custom role or an unknown capability, extra rows/columns render automatically. Capability names: recordDelivery, editCost, editPricing, stockTakeCount, stockTakeApprove, transferCreate, transferReceive, transferCancel, resolveDiscrepancy, manageUsers, editRefData, editSuppliers, viewReports, viewTransferHistory, deleteMovement, seeCost, seeSellingPrice, seeArchive, seeComparativeCharts, editAccessPolicy.
- **Per-account exceptions ("Always allow" / "Always block")** — Exceptions are keyed by USERNAME on both client and server (fixed as audit item AA-01 — an earlier version keyed by internal user id and silently did nothing). Server validation only accepts usernames matching letters/digits/underscore/dot/dash up to 64 chars; all current seed usernames pass, but an unusual username (an email address, a space) would be rejected as BAD_OVERRIDES.
- **"Ask for my password again when…" list (the sudo map)** — GOTCHA: the client only honours this list for 3 of the 10 rows (approve, resolve, delivery). Publish / user-admin / backup / access-policy ALWAYS prompt for the password regardless of the tick (index.html:4234 _sudoPrompt ignores the map), and archive / adjustment / cancel never prompt at all because the app never mints those proof types. So 7 of the 10 toggles currently change nothing on the device — though the server would honour them.
- **Locked items that can never be switched off (the floor)** — Server-side the floor is enforced twice: a policy that tries to relax it is REJECTED outright (FLOOR_VIOLATION), and even if a bad blob got through, the checker ignores it.
- **Publish / Activate button** — Today pressing it produces the toast 'Access-policy endpoint not configured yet (server phase pending)' — because the cloud address for it only arrives through the config channel, which has never worked. Fails closed and changes nothing.
- **Publishing and enforcing are two separate acts** — This is a DECISION and a written spec, not code. Nothing in the repo reads access_policy_enforce (grep: zero hits outside .md files). Today the only 'activation' that exists is policy-presence: the moment a policy blob is delivered, the client switches from its built-in defaults to the policy.
- **The master enforcement switch (access_policy_enforce)** — PARKED. The two doors it would arm (push-v2 and recordsteps-push) sit on the SharePoint connection shared with the live app, which is why this was treated as the one item worth being slow about. A companion client-side early-warning flag (_policyRequired) also does not exist in sync.js.
- **The policy itself (the blob) and its version number** — Shape: {version, roles, overrides, sudo, pin, pinEpoch}. Two copies exist server-side — access_policy_secure (with the PIN hash, never delivered) and access_policy (the device copy, PIN reduced to an expiry date).
- **Two Directors can't overwrite each other** — Audit fix AA-03. There is a known knock-on: the cloud writes the policy as three separate records, and a half-finished write can leave you temporarily unable to republish until it's reconciled (tracked as SRV-P3 in AZURE-CHUNK-AA-STAGING-LEDGER.md — fails safe, no bad policy ships).
- **The cloud checks the policy before saving it** — Deliberate design choice: reject, never silently correct — so someone probing the lock gets a hard no.
- **The one place the cloud decides yes or no (evaluateAccess)** — Deployed as a route on Function App bob-stock-money-fn (staging, 2026-07-09, per AZURE-CHUNK-AA-STAGING-LEDGER.md item 1 — not re-verified today). It is switched off in practice because almost nothing calls it yet, and no policy has ever been published for it to read.
- **The same rule engine on the device (Auth.can)** — Standing project rule P-13: anything the browser decides is tamperable. Roughly 60 button/screen checks across index.html and phase2.js route through Auth.can.
- **Built-in defaults when no policy exists (pre-activation)** — This is the ONLY part of the whole area that is live today — and it is live because it is the old behaviour, not the new feature.
- **The starting policy (what Activate actually publishes)** — Worth rehearsing before you press it — it is the one behaviour change staff will notice on day one.
- **See cost prices / See selling prices / See older data / See store-comparison charts** — IMPORTANT honesty point: only 'see cost prices' and 'see older data' can be genuinely enforced by the cloud (and those cloud doors are not applied yet). 'See selling prices' and the charts are screen-hiding only — the selling price is still sent to every device in the shared product list. The written enforcement matrix also still lists a fifth toggle, seeCharts, which was DROPPED from the code as unenforceable.
- **CSV exports respect the selling-price switch** — Added after an external auditor (Antigravity) spotted the inconsistency — logged as AA-EXT-1.
- **Taking a permission away cleans up what's already on the device** — Deliberately runs at DEVICE level, not per-person: if the policy arrives while nobody is logged in (the normal morning start-up), the cost data is wiped anyway rather than being left behind (audit item AA-04). Costs the store typed in itself are NOT wiped — only the centrally-supplied ones.
- **Backups never carry the access policy or hidden costs** — Audit items AA-28 and AA-04.
- **24-hour Stock Take PIN — Director sets and clears it** — DEFECT worth flagging: the Director's PIN panel (index.html:5851-5869) still reads ONLY the old local PIN. Under an active policy the badge would read 'No PIN Set' and the Clear PIN button would not appear, even though a policy PIN exists and Set/Clear correctly route through the policy. The helper that fixes this (_pinSource, index.html:5752) exists and is used on the other two PIN screens but not on this panel.
- **PIN checked by the cloud, not the device** — The cloud door that mints the pass (the user-verify Logic App's 'pin' operation) is item 10 on the staging apply list and is NOT applied. So today the PIN pass cannot be minted at all — only the old on-device PIN check works.
- **PIN-to-receive (the store account needs the PIN to receive an HO transfer)** — The PIN can only lift two things — doing a stock take and receiving a transfer. It can never lift anything else, on either side (PIN_CAPS, accessPolicy.js:34).
- **"Clear PIN" is an instant kill switch** — This answers AA-20, which is DECIDED and built. A pass also always self-expires within 24 hours regardless. Locking the screen also drops the unlock (S-244). RESOLVED: the deployed code implements instant revocation — grants carry a pinEpoch and clearing or changing the PIN bumps it, so every outstanding grant dies immediately (azure-functions/src/functions/accessPolicy.js:103-117; the app drops its local grant at sync.js:441-446). Decided by Kunal 2026-07-10 and recorded in AZURE-CHUNK-AA-LA-CHANGES.md.
- **Password re-prompts at the moment of a serious action** — Only 3 of the 10 listed actions are wired this way. The cloud has 12 recognised proof purposes (validateUser.js:30) — the app never mints archive, adjustment or cancel proofs.
- **Policy delivery to devices (and the PIN never leaving the cloud)** — BLOCKED BY A DEEPER PROBLEM: the settings download channel itself has never worked — sync.js:64 ships CONFIG_URL as the placeholder '%%CONFIG_URL%%' and _fetchRemoteConfig gives up at sync.js:691. So a policy could not reach a device even if one were published.
- **Devices notice a new policy quickly** — Pull echo is deployed on staging (echoes 0 while no policy exists).
- **Privileged actions done in a second browser tab still count** — Audit item AA-07. Proofs are never written to disk; logging out or locking wipes them (sync.js:214).
- **The cloud door that saves a policy (access-policy-write)** — Staging only. The live Logic Apps are untouched. A known imperfection (SRV-P3): it writes three records one after another and a half-completed write can temporarily block republishing — it fails safe.
- **The cloud doors that do NOT check the policy yet** — The staging ledger states plainly: these are 'not blocked on credentials, they are simply not done'. The client half of all of them IS built and sends the extra proof fields — old endpoints just ignore them, which is why nothing breaks today.
- **The archive era floor (never show a franchisee history from before they owned the store)** — A separate, working ownership-era engine DOES exist for the franchise-pricing work (azure-functions/src/functions/topology.js, 256 PASS in test/topology-proof.js) — but it is not wired to the archive door, and the AppConfig record the access-policy design names has never been created.
- **The safety nets around the rule engine (fail-closed behaviour)** — The Function also refuses to start answering at all if its secrets are not configured (returns a 500 so the caller fails closed rather than assuming yes).

</details>

## <a id="decisions"></a>Decisions

Why each one is here: a decision without its reasoning gets overturned by the next person
who does not know the reasoning. **rules out** is the column that stops re-litigation.

### DECIDED (339)

| # | Decision | Why | Rules out | Who / when | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| F-1 | Build the app in plain browser JavaScript with no framework and no build step. The files that sit in the repository are the exact files the browser downloads — nothing is compiled, packaged or transformed on the way out. | Nothing to keep alive. There is no toolchain that can rot, no build server, no versions to upgrade. Anyone (including a future auditor or a different AI) can open a file and read exactly what runs. The deploy workflow is 18 lines and explicitly turns the build off. IMPORTANT HONESTY NOTE: this reasoning is reconstructed from the code and the deploy configuration — no document anywhere actually records WHY this was chosen. It is an inherited decision. That is precisely the kind of thing that gets re-litigated, so it is worth Kunal writing the 'why' down once. | Rules out React, Vue, Angular, Svelte or any framework rewrite. Rules out TypeScript, npm packaging, bundlers and a CI build stage. Rules out 'just npm install that library' — every third-party file has to be deliberately copied into the repo (see F-14). Also means the app can be opened straight off a disk in an emergency. | inherited (original build, March 2026) — never formally recorded 2026-03-24 (first commit) | `C:\Users\joshi\repos\bob-stock\.github\workflows\deploy-swa.yml:18 (skip_app_build: true); CLAUDE.md:68; commit df0b21f / 898958f (2026-03-24); audit-artifacts/onedrive-imports/WAVE-DONE-CHECKLIST.md (line 5 — local working analysis, not in git) ('Stock has no build step')` |
| F-2 | The app is one screen that swaps its content in place (a single-page app), and the great majority of it lives in one very large file, index.html — 6,637 lines, about 1 megabyte. | CUT-9 | Every piece of server-phase work follows the same ten steps in order: write the scope, have outside auditors attack the scope BEFORE any code exists, map what already works, build the whole wave in one go on the test environment, prove it locally, run the full local break-it sweep, write the review pack, ground-truth every finding, fix them all in one batch, then loop until both auditors pass. Any change to this process gets WRITTEN INTO the process document, not just agreed in a chat. | Before this, work was reviewed only after it was built, which made every design defect cost a whole wave to unwind instead of a paragraph. Ratifying it in one document stops each session inventing its own order of work. The write-it-down clause exists because a process agreed only in conversation is invisible to the next session. | Rules out ad-hoc 'just build it and review after'; rules out reviewing code before the design has been attacked; rules out changing the process by agreeing something in chat and leaving the document behind (this rule has now been broken twice — see PROC one-cutover entry). | Kunal (ratified) 2026-07-03 | `AZURE-CHUNK-PROCESS.md:3-4 and :11-60; restated HANDOVER.md:187-217` |

Item 5's history is the proof — three review rounds, all on a spec, each one finding real defects that would otherwise have been built. | Rules out treating auditors as a final rubber stamp; rules out starting to build while the spec review is still out. | Kunal 2026-07-03 | `AZURE-CHUNK-PROCESS.md:16-18; HANDOVER.md:253-266` |

A write-isolated sandbox lets a change be proven against the REAL plumbing (same connector, same field formats) rather than a mock that can drift. | Rules out 'just try it on live and watch'. Rules out proving a server change with a mock or a local stub only — every chunk from 2 onward must show a real staging round-trip before it is called done. | Claude/Kunal 2026-06-23 | `AZURE-STAGING-ENVIRONMENT.md:3` |
| C0-2 | On staging, only small functional tests are allowed — never load tests or bulk seeding. | The staging Logic Apps share ONE SharePoint connection with the live ones. Heavy seeding by an auditor can throttle that shared connection and cause real failures on the live app. Also, every auditor's test rows must be prefixed (gpt_ / agy_) because the staging lists enforce unique keys, so unprefixed ids surface as the other auditor's false 'duplicate'. | Rules out asking an auditor to run a volume/performance test on staging. Rules out treating staging as fully isolated — it is isolated for DATA, not for capacity. | auditor-driven 2026-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK-PROCESS.md:72-77` |
| C1-1 | The columns the sync depends on must be indexed on a SharePoint list BEFORE that list grows past about 5,000 rows — indexes cannot be added afterwards. Only index columns that are actually filtered or sorted on server-side; every index costs write speed and there is a cap of about 20 per list. | SharePoint physically refuses to add an index once a list passes the threshold, so getting this wrong at list-creation time is permanent. This is the reason index planning is a named, tracked activity rather than an afterthought. | Rules out 'we'll add the index when it starts to hurt'. Rules out indexing columns speculatively. Makes list creation ORDER a cutover-critical step. | Claude/auditor-driven 2026-06-23 | `AZURE-INDEX-MANIFEST.md:3-5` |
| C1-3 | Even a real index cannot page through a result set bigger than 5,000 rows. The only thing that can is walking the list by its built-in row number (the ID cursor). So the ledger download was rebuilt to page by row ID instead of by timestamp. | This is the architectural reason the whole pull-hardening upgrade exists. SharePoint throttles any query whose MATCHED set exceeds ~5,000 regardless of indexing, which meant a brand-new device, or a device that had been offline a long time, would simply be unable to catch up once the live ledger passed 5,000 rows. Row ID is the primary key, always ordered, always available, and needs no custom index. | Rules out 'add more indexes' as the answer to scale. Rules out the old timestamp-based download cursor. Rules out any future server query designed to return more than 5,000 matching rows. | Claude + both auditors (Codex APPROVE-WITH-CONDITIONS, AGY APPROVE) 2026-06-23 | `AZURE-PULL-HARDENING-SPEC.md:6-18; AZURE-INDEX-MANIFEST.md:20` |
| C1-4 | Four safety rules ride with the ID-cursor download: (1) each cycle freezes a top row number at the start so new rows arriving mid-download cannot extend the walk forever; (2) each cycle re-reads the last 100 rows it already had, because SharePoint can make row 101 visible before row 100 and a naive cursor would skip 100 forever; (3) the saved position only moves forward once EVERY page has been safely stored; (4) a page that does not move the position forward aborts the cycle rather than quietly advancing. | Each of these is a silent-data-loss hole found by an auditor. The 100-row look-back is deliberately tied to the push concurrency of 50 (it is 2x that) — raise the push concurrency or add a bulk writer without raising the look-back first and the download starts missing rows. | Rules out changing push concurrency in isolation. Rules out any 'stop and advance' shortcut on a bad page. | auditor-driven (Codex Q1/Q4 + AGY #1), ratified after fixes 2026-06-23 | `AZURE-PULL-HARDENING-SPEC.md:22-29; AZURE-PULL-HARDENING-WAVE-REVIEW.md (implementation audit section)` |
| C1-5 | When the download switched to the new method, the server was built to accept BOTH the old and the new request shape, chosen automatically per request — not swapped over in one go. | An atomic swap would have trapped any device still running old cached code: it would send no row-id, the server would default to row 0, and the device would loop on page one forever, burning battery and Azure cost. The dual contract lets old devices keep working right through the cutover. | Rules out a same-URL hard cutover for any endpoint devices cache. Establishes the pattern for every later endpoint change. | auditor-driven (Codex P1 + AGY critical, the #1 blocker of that round) 2026-06-23 | `AZURE-PULL-HARDENING-WAVE-REVIEW.md (Implementation audit — dual-contract pull)` |
| C1-6 | Standing operational rule for the LIVE system: never edit or delete rows directly in the SharePoint list. Corrections must be made through the app, which appends a new row. | The download only ever walks forward by row number, so it can never see a change made to a row it has already passed. A direct edit or delete is invisible to every device that already synced past it. This is why deletions in the app are recorded as brand-new 'tombstone' rows rather than by removing anything. | Rules out fixing a bad row by hand in SharePoint. Rules out any future cloud code that UPDATEs or DELETEs a StockTransactions row in place. | auditor-driven (AGY #3) 2026-06-23 | `AZURE-PULL-HARDENING-SPEC.md:39-50` |
| C2-1 | Every stock row arriving from a device is re-checked by the server against the same rules the app applies, before it is written. The cloud does not trust the device. | Before this, any device (or anyone who had extracted a sync URL) could write any quantity, any type, any store, any product — including deliberately hostile text — straight into the ledger with no check at all. Client-side checks are a convenience control only (the permanent P-13 rule), so the wall has to be on the server. | Rules out relying on the app's own validation as the only gate. Rules out adding a new cloud write path without a matching server-side check. | Claude + both auditors (APPROVE-WITH-CHANGES, converged) 2026-06-24 | `AZURE-CHUNK2-SCOPE.md:14, :61` |
| C2-2 | The server must tell the truth about what it actually saved. It returns exactly which rows were accepted, which were duplicates, which were permanently rejected, and which failed temporarily — and it refuses the whole batch (saving nothing) if those four numbers do not add up to the number of rows sent. | The old server returned 'processed: N' where N was simply the number of rows SENT, not the number stored. A row that failed to insert was still reported as fine, the device marked it as synced, and the movement was silently lost forever. This was the single most dangerous gap found, and both auditors insisted it be fixed in the same change as the validation rather than after. | Rules out any endpoint that returns a bare 'ok'. Every write endpoint built since (record steps, catalogue) reuses this same four-bucket honest contract — that is now the house standard. | auditor-driven (GPT contract shape) + Claude 2026-06-24 | `AZURE-CHUNK2-SCOPE.md:10, :70-72, :84` |
| C2-3 | A rejected row goes to a durable cloud quarantine list AND is reported back to the device, which keeps it, flags it to an admin, and stops trying to re-send it. Rejected is permanent; failed is retried. | Kunal's rule was 'quarantine, never silently drop'. Splitting permanent rejections from temporary failures matters: a genuinely bad row retried forever is a loop, and a temporary network failure treated as a permanent rejection is a lost movement. An auditor blocked the first version precisely because only the device-side half existed, with no durable cloud record. | Rules out dropping a bad row. Rules out one undifferentiated 'error' bucket. | Claude + both auditors (AGY BLOCK on the missing cloud sink) 2026-06-24/29 | `AZURE-CHUNK2-SCOPE.md:66, :69, :72, :119` |
| C2-4 | If the product/store catalogue cannot be read at all, the server skips only the catalogue check (and says so loudly in its reply); but if the catalogue loads and the store or product is genuinely unknown, the row is REJECTED. | A configuration hiccup must not block the entire ledger — but a real unknown store or product is exactly the thing the check exists to catch. Failing open on a real miss would silently disable the control forever with nobody noticing. | Rules out blanket fail-open. Rules out blanket fail-closed on catalogue reads. Note the deliberate contrast with the auth check (C5-4), which fails CLOSED. | auditor-driven, adopted 2026-06-24 | `AZURE-CHUNK2-SCOPE.md:67` |
| C2-5 | Duplicate protection moved from 'look first, then insert' to letting SharePoint itself enforce that the transaction id is unique, and treating its rejection as 'already saved'. | The old check-then-insert had a race (two devices could both check, both find nothing, both insert) and did one extra database lookup per row at 50-at-a-time, risking throttling. Letting the database enforce uniqueness is atomic and removes both problems at once. Folded into Chunk 2 rather than kept separate because it is the same code path. | Rules out GET-then-insert dedup anywhere. Carries a hard precondition: the live list must be proven free of duplicate/blank transaction ids BEFORE the unique constraint is switched on (see open questions). | Claude + both auditors 2026-06-24 | `AZURE-CHUNK2-SCOPE.md:11, :17, :68` |
| C2-6 | Logic Apps have no pattern-matching (no regex), so the server's id safety check was rebuilt out of plain string operations that produce EXACTLY the same allowed characters as the app's own rule. No separate Azure Function was added for this. | A looser server rule would accept ids like 'abc.def' that the app itself would quarantine on the way back down — the server and the app would disagree, which is just a different flavour of silent data loss. The server must reject every id the app would. Eight specific 'not dangerous but client-unsafe' ids were made a formal acceptance bar to prove the equivalence. | Rules out a narrow 'block the dangerous characters' list. Rules out adding a Function to Chunk 2. Any future server-side id check must match the app's rule exactly, not approximately. | auditor-driven (both auditors chose Option A+) 2026-06-25 | `AZURE-CHUNK2-SCOPE.md:92-109` |
| C2-7 | Chunk 2 checks the SHAPE of data only. Who is allowed to write what (roles, stores, permissions) was deliberately kept out and pushed to Chunk 5. | Mixing shape validation with permission checks in one Logic App would have made both harder to reason about and audit. Keeping the boundary explicit also stopped anyone describing Chunk 2 as 'securing' the push, which it does not — it stops malformed data, not a hostile authorised device. | Rules out describing ingest validation as a security control. Rules out sneaking permission logic into the validation workflow. | Claude + both auditors 2026-06-24 | `AZURE-CHUNK2-SCOPE.md:54, :73` |
| C3-1 | There is no standalone Chunk 3. The double-receive problem was folded into Chunk 4. And the business rule was locked: if two devices receive the same transfer with the SAME quantity, it is silently de-duplicated; if they receive it with DIFFERENT quantities, it becomes a conflict that a Director must resolve by choosing which count is correct. | Kunal explicitly rejected 'whichever device syncs first wins', which was the engineering lean. A genuine disagreement about how much stock arrived is a business question, not a race to be settled by network timing. Making the Director choose needs cross-device record visibility and a resolution screen — which is exactly what Chunk 4 was building, so the two had to be built together. | Rules out first-sync-wins on receive quantities. Rules out building receive-idempotency as a standalone piece. Note: the old Q1-Q6 'leans' further down that same file, including the first-sync-wins lean at line 37, are now overruled and should not be read as live. | Kunal 2026-06-30 | `AZURE-CHUNK3-SCOPE.md:3-6` |
| C4-1 | Transfers, deliveries and stock-takes sync between devices as an append-only list of LIFE-EVENTS ('sent', 'received', 'flagged', 'resolved', 'counted', 'approved'), each one a permanent entry that is never edited. Each device replays those entries to rebuild the full record. One generic list for all three types — not a separate list per type, and not a mutable record that gets overwritten. | Records mutate through their life, which is the opposite of the stock ledger. Storing them as mutable blobs means two offline devices overwrite each other and there is no way to tell who was right. Making every step its own permanent entry removes the 'who overwrote whom' race entirely and reuses the sync machinery already proven on the ledger. Both auditors independently converged on a generic list; one picked append-only outright and the other called it 'more correct'. | Rules out one SharePoint list per record type (about 3x the build, plus schema churn). Rules out last-writer-wins on a whole-record blob. Rules out editing a synced record in place. | Kunal, after GPT + AGY architectural review 2026-06-22 | `AZURE-PHASE-SCOPE.md:67; AZURE-CHUNK4-SCOPE.md:55-61` |
| C4-2 | Stock numbers and record details are TWO SEPARATE streams. A record step never adds or removes stock — the stock effect always rides the existing ledger. They are linked by the transfer/record id. | This is the core safety invariant of the whole design. It means the new metadata stream physically cannot corrupt stock counts, and the proven ledger sync and validation are reused unchanged. Both auditors endorsed it as safer than putting lifecycle information into the stock ledger. | Rules out ever computing stock from record steps. Rules out putting quantities in a step that the app would then trust. | Claude + both auditors (D4-A) 2026-06-30 | `AZURE-CHUNK4-SCOPE.md:57-61, :136, :166` |
| C4-3 | Two different anti-duplicate keys doing two different jobs: the LEDGER key is fixed per transfer+store+product so a second receive can never double the stock; the STEP key deliberately includes a per-attempt marker so BOTH devices' receives survive and the disagreement stays visible. The attempt marker is created once and stored, so a crash-and-retry on the same device replays the same key rather than inventing a phantom second receive. | If both keys were fixed, the second receive would vanish and the quantity disagreement would be invisible — the exact thing Kunal decided must reach a Director. If the step key were a fresh random each time, one device tapping receive twice after a crash would look like two different people disagreeing. Both auditors changed the original design specifically to make the attempt marker stable and persisted. | Rules out a single shared idempotency key. Rules out a fresh random key per tap. Rules out keying on device id alone. | auditor-driven (both, D4-E APPROVE-WITH-CHANGES) 2026-06-30 | `AZURE-CHUNK4-SCOPE.md:98-101, :170; AZURE-CHUNK4-WAVE-REVIEW.md:26` |
| C4-4 | A record must never show a stock effect that did not actually land. The stock rows are sent FIRST and the matching record step is held back until those rows are confirmed saved; each step carries the list of ledger rows it expects, and shows as 'stock pending' or 'stock mismatch' until they are confirmed present. | This was the #1 gap both auditors flagged: a 'received' transfer whose stock rows had been rejected by validation would show a green tick to staff while the stock never moved. The first build got this half-right — it computed the state but still displayed the record as completed — and an auditor blocked it. | Rules out sending a record step before its stock. Rules out showing a completed record on unconfirmed stock. | auditor-driven (AGY + GPT, R1 mandatory) 2026-06-30 (fix 2026-07-01/02) | `AZURE-CHUNK4-SCOPE.md:180-186; AZURE-CHUNK4-WAVE-REVIEW.md:27, :51` |
| C4-5 | A transfer DRAFT is private to the device that made it and never syncs. The first thing other devices see is the 'submit' — the moment the transfer is actually sent. | Drafts are work-in-progress; propagating half-finished ones would be noise, and it matches the existing model where stock is deducted on submit, not on draft. | Rules out cross-device draft collaboration on transfers. | Claude (realised in code, flagged to auditors) 2026-07-01 | `AZURE-CHUNK4-WAVE-REVIEW.md:24` |
| C4-6 | Old records already sitting on devices are pushed up automatically, once, the first time the new code runs — never behind a button a person has to remember to press. If two devices hold genuinely different versions of the same record, that is flagged as a conflict, not silently resolved by whoever got there first. | Kunal's reasoning on the button was blunt: 'no one will remember'. There is no real production data yet, so at launch this is effectively a no-op — the machinery exists as a safety net so no record on a device is ever stranded. The conflict-not-first-writer-wins half was an auditor requirement, and the first build got it wrong (it deduplicated on the id and never compared the contents) until an auditor caught it. | Rules out a manual 'upload my old records' button. Rules out silent first-writer-wins on divergent history. | Kunal + auditor-driven (D4-I) 2026-06-30 (fix 2026-07-02) | `AZURE-CHUNK4-SCOPE.md:163, :174; AZURE-CHUNK4-WAVE-REVIEW.md:53` |
| C4-7 | The server checks the OUTSIDE of a record step (safe ids, known record and step types, size limits, and — new for deliveries — that money values are sane, positive, capped and to 2 decimal places). It does NOT re-implement the app's business rules on the inside of the step. | The server is a shape and safety backstop, not a second copy of the app. Over-validating the inside would reject legitimate shapes as the app evolves, and Logic Apps have no pattern matching anyway. Delivery money is the first money to reach the cloud, which is why the money check that was deliberately deferred from Chunk 2 lands here. Kunal chose to do the money check in a small Azure Function rather than in the Logic App. | Rules out full domain re-validation server-side. Note the money check FAILS OPEN if the Function is unreachable (the app's own check is the backstop) — deliberately the opposite of the auth check. | Claude + auditors (D4-G, D4-K); Function choice by Kunal 2026-06-30 / 2026-07-01 | `AZURE-CHUNK4-SCOPE.md:111-112, :172, :176; AZURE-CHUNK4-WAVE-REVIEW.md:55-56` |
| C4-8 | New delivery and stock-take ids get a random suffix so two devices can never mint the same id. Existing local ids are left alone. | Delivery and stock-take ids were plain millisecond timestamps. The moment those records started syncing, two devices creating a record in the same millisecond would collide and one record would overwrite the other. Rewriting existing ids was rejected because it would break their links to ledger rows. | Rules out timestamp-only ids for anything that syncs. Rules out retroactively renumbering existing records. | Claude + both auditors (D4-M) 2026-06-30 | `AZURE-CHUNK4-SCOPE.md:28, :148` |
| C4-9 | The two empty 'Transfers' and 'StockTakes' SharePoint lists left over from an earlier attempt are to be DELETED unused at phase cleanup, along with the dead V1 Logic Apps and a stray duplicate column — not repurposed. | They were scaffolded with a mutable-blob shape that the append-only design explicitly rejected, and were never wired to anything. Deleting them only after cutover verification and after confirming zero live references was an auditor condition. | Rules out reusing those lists. NOTE: the index manifest (AZURE-INDEX-MANIFEST.md:26) still says they will be 'replaced/repurposed' — that line is stale and contradicts this decision. | Claude + auditors (D4-J) 2026-06-30 | `AZURE-CHUNK4-SCOPE.md:118, :145, :175` |
| C5-1 | Three kinds of key: one per store (held by that store's devices), the head-office key, and a DIRECTOR key held only by Kunal and Shahin on their four personal devices. The Director key works on its own and covers every store — a Director device holds no store key at all. | Kunal rejected the earlier shortcut of treating head-office devices as if they were Directors: head-office staff must NOT be able to perform Director actions. Directors work from personal devices across all stores, which matches the app's existing director role, so tying the Director key to one store would fight the real workflow (both auditors agreed). Accepted risk, written down: Kunal and Shahin SHARE the key, so a leak means full write control of every store until it is rotated, and there is no way to tell which of the two acted. | Rules out 'head office device = Director'. Rules out binding the Director key to a store. Rules out Microsoft/Entra logins for this generation of the app (cost, shared store logins, offline-first). Rules out per-device registration for now — kept as a documented later upgrade on the same wire format. | Kunal (rejecting the round-1 design), ratified by both auditors 2026-07-04 | `AZURE-CHUNK5-SCOPE.md:29-38, :83-88, :170-176` |
| C5-2 | The server never stores a key. It stores a scrambled fingerprint of it, made with a per-row random value plus one secret 'pepper' that lives ONLY in the Azure Function's settings — never in SharePoint, never in the app, never in the repo. Rotation mints a new version and keeps accepting the old one until a grace date so offline devices are not locked out. | If the credential list itself leaks, the keys must still be useless. Keeping the pepper out of SharePoint means reading the whole credential list is not enough to forge anything. Dual-accept rotation from day one was an auditor requirement — without it, rotating a key strands any device that was offline at the time along with its unsynced movements. | Rules out storing keys anywhere in readable form. Rules out a hard rotation cut-off with no grace window. | auditor-driven (GPT counter, adopted) + Claude 2026-07-03/04 | `AZURE-CHUNK5-SCOPE.md:75-79, :128-130` |
| C5-3 | A named list of operations requires the Director key, not just a store key: stock adjustments (including stock-take approvals), recording or editing deliveries, approving/rejecting stock-takes, resolving or cancelling transfers, and cost changes. Ordinary movement deletion is deliberately NOT on the list. | These are the operations that move money or override someone else's count. Everything else only needs the store key, so day-to-day staff work is unaffected. Future cloud writes are born Director-gated by default: catalogue publishing, user management, supplier admin and pricing. | Rules out a store device performing a Director action. Rules out inferring 'this is a delivery' from free-text notes — delivery gating goes structurally through the record-step link, never through reading a reason field. | Kunal + both auditors (D2c, after a GPT counter and two amendments) 2026-07-04 | `AZURE-CHUNK5-SCOPE.md:83-100` |
| C5-4 | Key checking is done in one batched call to the Azure Function, which returns only yes/no and never any fingerprint material. If any required key is wrong — or the checking service is unreachable — the request is REFUSED. | Deliberately the opposite of the money check, which fails open. Money has a backstop in the app; authentication IS the wall, so 'we couldn't check' must mean 'no'. Batched into one call because a request can present two keys and two sequential calls would double the latency and cost and create awkward half-failures. It was proven by pulling the pepper out live and confirming the push then returned 401. | Rules out fail-open on auth. Rules out the server ever handing back hash material (which would make it a password-guessing oracle). Rules out per-credential sequential verify calls. | auditor-driven (both auditors), Claude built 2026-07-04 | `AZURE-CHUNK5-SCOPE.md:119-127; AZURE-CHUNK5-WAVE-REVIEW.md:36` |
| C5-5 | Checking an overall rule about a group of rows is NOT a substitute for checking each individual row that a request points at. The transfer gate had to be rebuilt to look up EVERY ledger row a step references and confirm each one belongs to that transfer and to one of its two stores. | The first fix checked only rows already TAGGED with the transfer id. An auditor then smuggled in an UNTAGGED foreign row and had it accepted — the same hole a second time. This is the single most repeated lesson on this project: an aggregate invariant misses anything outside the grouping key. A genuinely missing row is treated as 'try again later', never as 'fine'. | Rules out satisfying a per-item requirement with a grouped check. Rules out treating a missing referenced row as confirmation. | auditor-driven (GPT blocked twice on the same fix family), ground-truthed and fixed by Claude 2026-07-04 | `AZURE-CHUNK5-WAVE-REVIEW.md:104-119` |

Every refusal returns a self-explaining message so even an old cached copy of the app can tell the user why. | The bootstrap paradox — if you turn enforcement on before every device has a key, every device is locked out at once with no way to be told why. An auditor raised this as a counter in round 1 and it was adopted. | Rules out flipping enforcement on in one step. Rules out a bare 401 with no explanation. | auditor-driven (GPT counter, adopted) + Kunal 2026-07-03/04 | `AZURE-CHUNK5-SCOPE.md:112-115` |
| C6-1 | Catalogue changes reach the stores when a Director presses a 'Review & Publish' button — not automatically on every edit. The screen shows how many unpublished changes there are, a preview of exactly what will change, and when it was last published. | Kunal is the main editor and edits are infrequent. Auto-publish would push a mistyped price company-wide the instant it was typed. 'Make my changes, then publish' matches how the work is actually done. Auto-publish stays available as a later change if the button becomes a nuisance. | Rules out publish-on-every-keystroke. Rules out publishing without a preview. | Kunal (Claude lean, both auditors agreed) — D3 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK6-SCOPE.md:44-51, :105; AZURE-CHUNK6-WAVE-REVIEW.md:41` |
| C6-2 | A publish sends ONLY the rows that actually changed, each stamped with the version it was based on. The server merges them one row at a time and rejects a row whose server copy has moved on since. Publishing a full snapshot of the local catalogue is forbidden. | Merging by id alone still loses data two ways: two publishes reading the same starting point both write the whole file and one wins; and a device publishing its FULL local snapshot silently overwrites rows it never touched but which someone else has since updated. An auditor then proved a worse version of this live — a publish that was correctly REJECTED by the server still reported the row as 'accepted', so the app cleared it and the edit vanished. The fix retries the read-merge-write until it genuinely lands and reports success only after it has persisted. | Rules out whole-blob replacement. Rules out trusting an 'accepted' list that was produced before the write persisted. | auditor-driven (GPT, the stronger of the two), adopted + hardened after a P1 block 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK6-SCOPE.md:113-123; AZURE-CHUNK6-WAVE-REVIEW.md:88-96` |
| C6-3 | THE COST STRIP. What BOB pays for a product is removed entirely from the shared catalogue that everyone receives. Cost is served through a separate, guarded channel available only to Directors and to corporate (company-owned) stores. A franchisee's device never receives it and keeps its own cost, which the franchisee enters themselves. | Kunal's business rule is that a franchisee's cost is genuinely different — they buy at the price head office charges them — so one company-wide cost is simply wrong for them and must never overwrite theirs. But BOTH auditors countered the original plan of 'send the cost and have franchisee devices ignore it': that is a privacy LEAK, because a franchisee can read the shared file directly and extract BOB's costs and margins. The rule that came out of it is now a general principle on this project: security and privacy rules must be enforced by the server; a client-side 'ignore' is not enforcement. A related hole was found and closed in the same round — the config endpoint was quietly handing the whole cost file to a franchise key. | Rules out putting cost in the shared catalogue at all. Rules out client-side 'ignore the field' as a privacy control. Also fixes what happens when a store changes hands: corporate-to-franchise stops central cost and prompts a local cost review; franchise-to-corporate starts receiving it. | Kunal (business rule) + both auditors (COUNTER on the mechanism), adopted 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK6-SCOPE.md:66-83, :125-137; AZURE-CHUNK6-WAVE-REVIEW.md:98-100` |
| C6-4 | Only the Director key may publish catalogue changes to the cloud. Widening it to the head-office store key later is a small, reversible, one-endpoint change with no data migration. | The two auditors split on this. The deciding factor was who actually does product data entry: if it is Kunal and Shahin, Director-only is fine; if it is warehouse staff, they need a path. Kunal chose to start tight. The trade-off written down: head-office-originated catalogue edits stay local and unpublished until a Director re-enters and publishes them. | Rules out a store key publishing catalogue changes. WARNING: the same file at :139-149 still presents this as 'the one OPEN decision for Kunal' — it is decided; the open version is stale. | Kunal (D-HOAUTH), auditors split 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK6-SCOPE.md:109` |

If a device could mint it, one bad device could either freeze everyone's catalogue or force endless re-downloads. | Rules out client-supplied version numbers on any published payload. | Claude, stated to auditors (D-VERSION), no dissent 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK6-SCOPE.md:85-87` |
| C6-6 | The rule 'you cannot deactivate a product or category that still has stock' stays enforced in the app only, NOT on the server. | Deliberately owned and documented. It is a tidiness rule, not a security rule: the only person who could bypass it is a trusted Director using developer tools, the effect (hiding something) is reversible and loses no data, and enforcing it server-side would mean summing the whole ledger on every publish — a permanent runtime cost on a shared connection for negligible benefit. The principle it established: security and privacy rules MUST be server-side; a Director-only, reversible tidiness rule may be client-side. Flagged for revisit only if per-account permissions let less-trusted roles deactivate. | Rules out re-raising this as a server-side gap. Gives a written test for where a rule belongs. The revisit trigger (Chunk 9 per-account auth) has now happened — see open questions. | Kunal + Claude, after a GPT counter; both auditors accepted 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK6-WAVE-REVIEW.md:104-109` |
| C7-1 | Chunk 7 splits in two: the app-side hardening and small carried code items were built now; ALL secret rotation and relocation (deploy token, function secrets to Key Vault, rotating the sync URLs) was deferred to a single security pass executed as the last step before the live cutover. | You rotate a secret AT the boundary you are protecting against — right before the app leaves single-owner alpha. Everything was on staging and uncommitted, so rotating early would just mean rotating again at cutover. Kunal specifically chose to include the deploy token in the deferred pass rather than doing it early, accepting that it sits in the private repo until then. | Rules out ad-hoc secret rotation mid-build. Creates a hard dependency: the cutover security checklist MUST be executed, or none of it happens. | Kunal (D7-1, D7-2) 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK7-SCOPE.md:83-91` |
| C7-2 | Chart.js, Dexie and the three Google fonts were downloaded into the app itself, and the browser security policy was tightened to drop all three outside internet addresses. Inline scripts and inline styles were deliberately KEPT allowed. | Removing outside dependencies removes a whole class of supply-chain risk and makes the app fully self-contained. But both auditors countered the original plan to go fully strict: the app uses inline scripts, inline onclick handlers and inline styles throughout, so a strict policy would simply break the rendering. A proper nonce-based policy is a separate future refactor, not this chunk. Both auditors byte-verified the downloaded copies against the originals. | Rules out loading anything from a third-party address. Rules out an auditor flagging 'unsafe-inline' as an unowned finding — it is a documented, both-auditor-endorsed decision. | Kunal (D7-3) + both auditors (COUNTER on keeping inline) 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK7-SCOPE.md:29-41; AZURE-CHUNK7-WAVE-REVIEW.md:11-21, :81` |
| C7-3 | Only the Latin character set of the fonts was bundled — 10 files instead of Google's 54. | Appropriate for a Western Australian English app. But both auditors banked a launch-gating condition: if ANY staff member, store or product name uses non-Latin characters, the font set must be regenerated before launch or those names will not render properly. | Rules out adding a non-Latin name without regenerating the fonts. This is a launch condition hiding inside what looks like a cosmetic choice. | Claude, condition added by both auditors 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK7-WAVE-REVIEW.md:83-85` |
| C7-4 | Categories and product types can no longer be hard-deleted. 'Delete' now means 'deactivate' — the row stays, publishes normally, disappears from the pick-lists for new items, but its label still shows on historical records that point at it. The server refuses to attach a new product to a deactivated category. | Hard deletes never propagated to other devices at all (the sync only ever adds or updates, it never removes), so a category deleted on the office PC lived on forever on every store phone. Converting delete to deactivate makes the change actually travel. Keeping the label visible means old records do not suddenly display blanks. | Rules out hard deletion of catalogue reference data. Rules out expecting a removal to sync. | Kunal (D7-4, both auditors agreed) + GPT's inactive-semantics detail 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK7-SCOPE.md:68-78, :86; AZURE-CHUNK7-WAVE-REVIEW.md:31-39` |
| C7-5 | On top of checking that a referenced stock row belongs to the right transfer and store, the server now also checks that the row is the right KIND for that step (a 'submit' may only point at a transfer-out, a 'receive' at a transfer-in, and so on). | Carried forward from the Chunk-5 audit as agreed hardening. The original shorthand for this map was wrong — a legitimate resolve ALSO produces transfer-in rows for flagged settlements and returns, not only adjustments. Both auditors corrected it against the actual code before it was built. | Rules out a context-correct but semantically wrong row being accepted. | auditor-driven (both, carried from the C5 review) 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK7-SCOPE.md:57-67; AZURE-CHUNK7-WAVE-REVIEW.md:25-29` |
| C8-1 | Ledger archival is BUILT NOW and ships in the single cutover — Kunal overruled the engineering recommendation to design it now and build it later. | Claude's lean was to design it, get it audit-approved, then prioritise the two real pre-launch blockers and build archival later as an add-on, because the growth ceiling was months away. Kunal chose to have everything done and audited before launch in one deploy rather than a string of later deploys. | Rules out treating archival as post-launch work. Note: the growth reasoning that framed the urgency is now void — the app is not in use and nobody is logging transactions (HANDOVER.md:528-535) — but the build-now decision stands and the work is done. | Kunal (D8-0, choosing Option A over Claude's lean B) 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:23-37, :140` |
| C8-2 | You can never simply delete old movements, because stock is the SUM of every movement ever made. Old movements are only removed after their balance has been captured in an immutable snapshot: work out the balance per store and product, prove it, save it, copy the movements to an archive list, verify the copy, and only then remove them from the live list. | This is the founding invariant of the whole chunk. Deleting a movement without snapshotting first silently changes the stock number. Everything else in Chunk 8 exists to make that sequence provably safe. | Rules out ever pruning the ledger by date or age alone. Rules out storing a current stock level anywhere as the authority. | Claude, endorsed by both auditors 2026-07-05 | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:39-50` |
| C8-3 | THE CRUX: the archival cut-off point is the SharePoint row number, not a business date. A movement that was entered late or offline for an old date gets a NEW higher row number, so it lands on the 'keep' side and is counted — never silently swallowed by the archive. | Both auditors independently identified date-based cut-off as a data-loss bug. A movement dated last month but synced today would fall on the archived side of a date boundary, after the snapshot had already been computed without it — the stock would be quietly wrong and there would be no trace. Row numbers only ever go up, so a late arrival can never fall behind the line. The business date is still kept on the row for reporting. | Rules out any date-based archival boundary. This decision is cited BY LINE NUMBER from the org-structure work, so editing above line 87 of the Chunk-8 scope silently breaks that reference. | auditor-driven (both raised it; the crux fix), adopted 2026-07-06 | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:87-91; AZURE-CHUNK8-WAVE-REVIEW.md:19-21` |
| C8-4 | Archiving runs when the live ledger reaches about 5,000 rows OR six months have passed, whichever comes FIRST. Archived movements move to a separate archive list, not a flag on the existing one. | Kunal picked a hybrid over both auditors' suggestions (one wanted annual, the other a rolling 12 months). The 5,000 trigger is the SharePoint threshold itself, so it is a genuine safety valve that self-paces to how busy the business is; the six-month cap gives a predictable rhythm aligned to end-of-financial-year reporting. LOAD-BEARING: the 5,000 trigger is the only thing keeping the archive run's single-page read safe — the run reads at most 5,000 rows in one go and refuses if there are more. Paging past that was documented but never built. | Rules out relaxing the cadence without first building paging into the archive run. Rules out an archived-flag-on-the-same-list design. | Kunal (D8-1), overriding both auditors' picks 2026-07-06 | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:125-129, :141; AZURE-CHUNK8-WAVE-REVIEW.md:47-48` |
| C8-5 | A device must NEVER see a half-finished archive run — an old snapshot alongside a shrunk live ledger would show wrong stock. The run holds a lock, and the new snapshot version is published LAST, after the archive copy is durable and verified. | Both auditors independently rated this the highest severity item in the chunk. A device catching the system mid-run would compute stock from a snapshot that does not cover the rows that have already been removed. | Rules out publishing the snapshot before the archive is verified. Rules out running archival without a lock. | auditor-driven (both, P0), adopted 2026-07-06 | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:97-99, :118-120; AZURE-CHUNK8-WAVE-REVIEW.md:21-22` |
| C8-6 | Before anything is archived, the run must prove it changes no stock number: every store-and-product pair must add up identically before and after (including products with no recent movement), and the archive copy must match the source by both count and content fingerprint. On ANY mismatch it publishes nothing, archives nothing and leaves the live ledger untouched. A device with unsynced local movements does not switch to the snapshot until they have drained. | Kunal's top stated priority is robustness. An auditor countered the original migration gate as 'too thin' — balance-neutrality alone was not enough without freezing writes, covering zero-movement products, and hashing both copies. A later round found the hash proved only that the same ROWS were there, not that their CONTENTS were intact, and it was upgraded to a content hash that the run re-reads and re-checks before it publishes or deletes anything. | Rules out an archive run that 'mostly' balances. Rules out identity-only verification. | auditor-driven (GPT COUNTER on D8-5, then GPT-2 at the code audit) 2026-07-06, hardened at the code audit | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:112-120; audit-artifacts/CHUNK8-AUDIT-RESPONSE.md (GPT-2)` |
| C8-7 | Any report whose date range reaches back into archived history must either load the archive or refuse with a clear 'archive required' message. It must never quietly return a smaller number. When a Director does load the archive, those rows are spliced into the working data so EVERY report and export sees them, rather than each report being wired up individually. | Kunal's requirement was explicit: reports and charts — including the end-of-financial-year report that spans the archive boundary — must stay available. A silent under-count in a sales or wastage report is worse than an error message, because nobody notices. An auditor then found that about ten report and CSV paths read the raw data directly and would have bypassed any per-report wiring, so the overlay approach was adopted instead. | Rules out a silent under-count. Rules out wiring archive awareness report-by-report. | Kunal (requirement) + both auditors (fail-closed, P1) + AGY-2 fix 2026-07-06, fixed at the code audit | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:108-111; AZURE-CHUNK8-WAVE-REVIEW.md:30-31; audit-artifacts/CHUNK8-AUDIT-RESPONSE.md (AGY-2)` |
| C8-8 | Archiving touches the stock ledger ONLY. A completed old transfer whose stock rows have been archived must still show as confirmed, not regress to 'stock pending'. Whether an archived row counts as confirmed is decided by a real per-row archive index, not by comparing dates. | Without this, archiving would make every old completed transfer suddenly look unfinished. The first build used a date shortcut — if the step was older than the cut-off, assume its rows were archived — and an auditor showed this falsely cleared rows that had NEVER landed, breaking the Chunk-4 guarantee that a record never claims a stock effect that did not happen. This is the same aggregate-instead-of-per-item trap as C5-5, one chunk later. | Rules out a date heuristic standing in for per-row proof. Rules out archiving record steps alongside ledger rows. | auditor-driven (both, P1; then GPT-1 at the code audit) 2026-07-06, fixed at the code audit | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:100-103; audit-artifacts/CHUNK8-AUDIT-RESPONSE.md (GPT-1)` |
| C8-9 | Uniqueness of transaction ids must be checked across BOTH the live and the archive lists. A movement that has already been folded into a published snapshot can no longer be deleted in the app. Accepted residual, written down: a deletion arriving in a LATER archive cycle for an already-snapshotted movement cannot retroactively correct that snapshot — this is managed by habit (do not delete long-archived movements) rather than by code. | Once a movement is inside a snapshot balance, deleting it would need the snapshot recomputed, which is not something a store device can do. Blocking the delete is honest; allowing it would create a phantom deletion that changes nothing. The residual is money-affecting and is recorded in exactly one place, in a document that has no external-audit section on it. | Rules out checking uniqueness against the live list alone. Rules out deleting an archived movement through the app. | auditor-driven (both, P1; AGY-3 for the delete block) 2026-07-06, delete-block added at the code audit | `AZURE-CHUNK8-SCOPE.md:104-106; AZURE-CHUNK8-WAVE-REVIEW.md:38-42; audit-artifacts/CHUNK8-AUDIT-RESPONSE.md (AGY-3)` |
| C8-10 | Reading archived movements requires either the Director key or the head-office store key. A franchisee cannot pull other stores' archived history. | The archive carries every store's historical movements in one place, so it is exactly the kind of thing store-level privacy is meant to protect. Restricting it to Director/head-office was the interim gate; proper franchise scoping over the archive was noted as a Chunk-10 concern. | Rules out a franchise store key reading the archive. | Claude, flagged to the audit 2026-07-06 | `AZURE-CHUNK8-WAVE-REVIEW.md:43-44` |
| PPL-1 / D9-account-model | Logins belong to a ROLE AND A PLACE, not to a person. Each store gets two accounts — a shared shop-floor account and a store-manager account — plus territory manager, franchisee, Head Office and Director accounts. Around 15-40 accounts in total. Staff still TYPE their name on each movement, but that name is a label, not a login. | Staff cannot be logging in and out all day on a shared shop-floor device, and Kunal did not want to administer a password for every individual. Accountability comes from the append-only movement history, not from personal passwords. | Rules out individual staff logins and any per-person staff directory. Rules out ever claiming in a report or an audit that 'person X personally did this' — the honest wording is 'authenticated as account X from device/store Y'. Also rules out designs that need a device to hold a roster of people (it only ever needs its own one account). | Kunal 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:136-162` |
| C9-NO-M365 | Staff accounts stay app-managed. No Microsoft 365 / Entra / MSAL logins for the Stock App. | Kunal's hard constraint: no per-user licences, no new monthly cost. The existing Function + Logic App + SharePoint plumbing can do server-side password checking for free. | Rules out 'just use Microsoft logins' as an answer to any future auth problem — including single sign-on, Microsoft-hosted password resets, and email/SMS reset infrastructure. | Kunal 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:4-7 and :225` |
| C9-TWO-LAYER | Two separate locks, both of which must open for a sensitive action: the DEVICE must be an authorised device (Chunk 5 key), AND the PERSON must be a verified account (Chunk 9 proof). | A device key alone cannot tell Kunal apart from a staff member holding Kunal's unlocked phone. A person login alone would leave an unknown device talking to the cloud. Keeping both means a stolen password is useless without an authorised device and vice-versa. | Rules out replacing the device keys with a person login, and rules out treating a person login on its own as authorisation. It is also why the store-scoping in Chunk 10 could be hung on the device credential rather than on the person. | Kunal / Claude, converged with auditors 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:44-48; AZURE-CHUNK9-WAVE-REVIEW.md:22` |
| PPL-2 | The password travels only twice: when you log in, and when the app re-asks for it before a sensitive action. After that the server hands back a short-lived signed 'pass' (12 hours for a session, 5 minutes for a specific sensitive action) and it is the pass, not the password, that rides along with each request. | Sending the password with every sensitive request means it sits in memory and in more places than necessary. A short-lived pass, tied to the device it was issued on, cannot be replayed from another device and dies instantly when a password is reset. | Rules out the original design of attaching the password to each sensitive request. Rules out long-lived tokens, cross-device reuse of a pass, and any grace period after a password change. | Codex audit finding, adopted by Kunal 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:168-177 (supersedes §3.2); AZURE-CHUNK9-WAVE-REVIEW.md:23-25` |
| PPL-3 / D9-1 + D9-2 | The person check applies ONLY to the admin actions that already need internet — publishing the catalogue, reading corporate costs, running or pulling the archive, and user administration. Everyday shop-floor work (logging stock in and out, receiving, counting) needs the device key only. User administration is Director-only; Head Office can look but not change. | The app is offline-first and staff must be able to work with no signal. The sensitive actions were already online-only, so requiring a cloud person-check there costs nothing operationally. | Rules out putting person authentication in front of everyday stock movements — that would break offline working, which is non-negotiable. Rules out Head Office creating or resetting accounts. | Kunal 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:68-71 and :107-109` |
| PPL-4 | The staff/account list is delivered privately and trimmed to what each device needs. Director and Head Office devices see all active accounts; a store or franchise device sees only accounts connected to its own store. It is never published in the open catalogue payload that every device downloads. | Both external auditors independently rejected the original plan (publish the directory inside the shared catalogue) as a privacy leak — a franchise device would learn every other store's staff and roles. | Rules out putting the account list into the shared master_data catalogue. Rules out any design that needs a full company account list on a store device. | Codex + Antigravity, adopted 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:184-187 (supersedes §3.1)` |
| PPL-5 / D9-3 | Five wrong passwords locks the account for 15 minutes, then 30, 60, 120. A Director can unlock early. Crucially, a lockout must be VISIBLE: the user-admin screen shows who is locked and how many failures, and one email goes out per lockout event (not per attempt). | Kunal: 'I need to know this has happened to someone, so if it is malicious I can do something about it.' A silent lockout tells him nothing. | Rules out silent lockouts, and rules out an email per failed attempt (which would let someone email-bomb the business by typing wrong passwords in a loop). | Kunal 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:110-116; AZURE-CHUNK9-WAVE-REVIEW.md:27-28` |
| PPL-6 | The device is checked FIRST, and a failed password only counts against an account if the device was entitled to try that account in the first place. A store device can only attempt its own store's account or its own users — it can never reach a Director account, so it can never lock Kunal out. Directors themselves can still be locked (a stolen Director device gets five tries). If BOTH Directors are locked, the recovery is a manual SharePoint edit using the Microsoft 365 admin account. | Without this, anyone holding any store device could deliberately lock the owner out of his own admin screens by spamming wrong passwords. Fixing it with a blanket 'Directors never lock' would have handed a stolen Director device unlimited guesses. Checking the device first solves both. | Rules out a Director exemption from lockout. Rules out counting failed attempts before the device has been authenticated. | Auditor-driven (Antigravity + Codex), reconciled; the internal deep audit then found it had NOT actually been built and fixed it 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:188-200; AZURE-CHUNK9-WAVE-REVIEW.md:57-61; commit 8ed5c22` |
| PPL-7 / D9-4 | Kunal sets and keeps every password. No self-service password choosing, no forced password change on first login. Minimum length 8, or 10 for the two Director accounts. Auditors are told to treat 'the owner knows all passwords' as an accepted business decision, not a finding. | Kunal's words: 'I always know everyone's passwords.' It is a small business with shared shop-floor devices; recovering a forgotten staff password must not need infrastructure. | Rules out self-service resets, must-change-on-first-login flows, and email/SMS reset plumbing. Rules out any audit report claiming password secrecy from the Directors, or claiming personal non-repudiation. | Kunal 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:117-122 and :211-212` |
| AA-PIN-SERVER | The 24-hour PIN stops being a local check on the device and becomes a grant issued by the server: entering the PIN gets a signed, time-limited permission that the cloud itself recognises. It lifts exactly two things — running a stock take and receiving Head Office transfers. Clearing or changing the PIN instantly kills every grant already out there. | The PIN as built was a hash comparison inside the browser, so it protected nothing against a tampered device, and it granted stock-take only — not the receive-transfer behaviour Kunal asked for. Making it a server grant is the only way the cloud can honour or refuse it. | Rules out the browser-only PIN check, and rules out letting a shop-floor account receive Head Office transfers without a PIN. Also rules out grants that outlive a cleared PIN (Kunal chose instant kill over letting them expire). | Kunal 2026-07-09 (PIN-to-receive) / 2026-07-10 (AA-20 instant kill) | `AZURE-CHUNK-ACCOUNT-ACCESS-SCOPE.md:113-115 and :162-166; azure-functions/src/functions/accessPolicy.js:171-196` |
| PPL-9 | For offline login the device keeps a separate, deliberately slow-to-crack local check value (PBKDF2, 210,000 rounds, a random salt unique to that device). The real cloud password hash never leaves SharePoint. If the local value is deleted there is no fallback — you must be online to log in again — and it is stripped out of every backup file. | A stolen device or backup must not hand over anything that works against the cloud. A separate device-local value means the worst case is a slow, device-specific crack, not access to the account. | Rules out storing the cloud password hash on the device, rules out a weaker 'just let them in' fallback when the local value is missing, and rules out backups carrying login material. | Codex + Antigravity, adopted 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:201-205; AZURE-CHUNK9-WAVE-REVIEW.md:29-30` |
| PPL-10 | Chunk 9 was shipped ASLEEP: the two new cloud addresses are deliberately missing from the app's configuration, so the app still uses the old local login. But catalogue publishing and archiving ALREADY demand the new pass. So at switch-on, the app and the configuration must be flipped in the same moment. | Shipping it dormant meant nothing could break before it was proven. The catch is the half-and-half state: turn on one side without the other and catalogue publishing stops working. | Rules out enabling either half on its own, and rules out treating the Chunk 9 switch-on as a low-risk config tweak. | Claude (build decision), recorded for Kunal 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-WAVE-REVIEW.md:46-48` |
| PPL-11 / D9-7 | Stock-take approval and discrepancy resolution should eventually happen ON THE SERVER, not in the browser. Kunal: 'I ultimately don't want anything in the browser that is not in the cloud.' Deferred out of Chunk 9, but recorded as a genuine commitment, not a nice-to-have. | Today a tampered device could in principle write the underlying approval rows itself. Moving the write server-side is the only real fix; it was too big for Chunk 9. | Rules out closing this out as 'accepted risk' — Kunal explicitly asked for it to stay on the books. | Kunal 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:130-134; SERVER-SIDE-REQUIREMENTS.md:49-56` |
| PPL-12 / D9-8 | Director-only stock actions (approving a stock take, resolving or cancelling a transfer, recording a delivery, making an adjustment) require INTERNET AT THE MOMENT you do them: the app gets a purpose-specific pass, attaches it to the resulting rows, and the cloud refuses those row types if the pass is missing or invalid. Ordinary store work is unaffected. NOT BUILT — the cloud never actually checks this today. | Kunal accepted the online-only cost for this small set of actions because it is the earliest way to stop a tampered device inventing Director-level records. It is a partial down-payment on the full server-side write commitment. | Rules out Directors approving stock takes or resolving transfers offline. It does NOT yet rule anything out in practice, because the cloud-side check was never wired. | Kunal 2026-07-07 (decided); confirmed unbuilt 2026-07-30 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:217-222; AZURE-CHUNK-AA-ENFORCEMENT-MATRIX.md:23-26 (finding D4)` |
| C9-PROOF-NO-SELF-CERT | A pass never carries its own authority. When the cloud checks a pass it looks up the account's CURRENT role in SharePoint rather than believing the role written inside the pass, and the role named in a request is never trusted as permission. Related standing rules from the same audit round: the last remaining Director can never be deactivated or demoted, and a locked account stays locked even if the lock value is stored in an odd format. | If the pass could vouch for its own role, anyone who obtained one could keep acting as a Director after being demoted. The last-Director guard exists because deactivating the only Director would brick the admin screen with no way back in. | Rules out role information travelling from client to server as authorisation, and rules out any 'trust the token's claims' shortcut in future server work. | Codex audit + internal deep audit, adopted 2026-07-07 | `commit 9637860; AZURE-CHUNK9-WAVE-REVIEW.md:62-66` |
| C9-BOOTSTRAP | The very first Director account is created by a one-time seeding path that is then disabled, and a brand-new app install with no account list refuses all sensitive actions rather than allowing them. | You cannot create the first admin using the admin screen that requires an admin. The one-shot path solves it without leaving a permanent back door. | Rules out leaving the seeding path permanently open, and rules out a fresh device 'failing open' before it has synced. | Codex, adopted 2026-07-07 | `AZURE-CHUNK9-SCOPE.md:213-215` |
| ISO-1 / D10-1 | Which stores' data you can see is decided by the ACCOUNT you logged into, and that store list is fixed and held in the cloud. The server reads it only from the verified credential row — never from anything the app sends. Store account sees its one store; territory manager sees their stores; a franchisee sees their stores plus their franchise office; Head Office and Directors see everything. If the scope cannot be determined, the server sends NOTHING. | Kunal confirmed accounts are fixed identities tied to a place — nobody logs into another account 'inside' a store account, and a territory manager uses their own device. That single clarification collapsed a whole planned layer of device-versus-account intersection logic. | Rules out cross-login (logging in as a different role on a store device), rules out the app telling the server which stores it wants, and rules out defaulting to 'send everything' when something is unclear. | Kunal 2026-07-08 | `AZURE-CHUNK10-SCOPE.md:46-51 and :79-82; AZURE-CHUNK10-WAVE-REVIEW.md:33-38` |
| ISO-2 | Store isolation is not just about what you can READ — the cloud also REJECTS any attempt to WRITE a row for a store outside your account's list. The old rule ('your rows must match your one store') was widened to 'your rows must be inside your store list'. | Read-only isolation would still let a tampered device manufacture movements in another store's books. The old single-store rule had to be widened or a multi-store franchisee could not save anything at all — its rows belong to its member stores, not to the account itself. | Rules out treating Chunk 10 as 'just a download filter'. Rules out the earlier absolute one-store write rule from Chunk 5. | Codex + Antigravity audit, adopted 2026-07-08 | `AZURE-CHUNK10-WAVE-REVIEW.md:46-47 and :55-59` |
| ISO-TRANSFER-CRUX | A transfer between two stores is stored as two separate legs, each owned by one store. So plain store filtering already gives the receiving store its own leg — no special 'either end' logic is needed on the movement ledger. The 'either end' rule is only needed on the transfer PAPERWORK (the record steps), so a receiving store can see who sent what and when. | This was flagged as the hardest part of the design and was expected to need complicated rules. Building it revealed the data already worked that way, which made the whole thing simpler and safer. | Rules out re-opening 'how does a receiver see an incoming transfer without seeing the sender's data' — it is settled. Rules out adding either-end logic to the ledger filter. | Claude (discovered during build), auditor-reviewed 2026-07-08 | `AZURE-CHUNK10-WAVE-REVIEW.md:49-54` |
| ISO-6 / D10-2 + D10-6 + D10-7 + D10-8 | Minimum necessary sharing: a receiving store sees an incoming transfer's details (who sent it, what, when) but none of the sender's other movements; a store sees Head Office transfers to itself but not Head Office's stock; territory managers and franchisees see full individual movement detail for THEIR stores. Store NAMES stay visible to everybody — only DATA is scoped. | Franchisees pay for and trade their own stock, so they need full detail on their own stores and nothing beyond. Kunal judged the mere existence of other store names to be non-sensitive in a franchise chain — hiding them would have complicated the app for no real gain. | Rules out scoping the store directory itself. Rules out giving a receiving store the sender's balances, costs or unrelated activity. | Kunal 2026-07-08 | `AZURE-CHUNK10-SCOPE.md:83, :91-94 and :137-140` |
| ISO-3 | When the app does not yet know its store scope, it fails CLOSED: it falls back to the stores on the logged-in account, and if even that is unknown it treats nothing as allowed — no opening balances are seeded, and a backup import strips everything. | The first build failed OPEN in that window, which both auditors rated critical: a device could briefly hold and display another store's data before the first scope message arrived. | Rules out any 'allow until we know better' behaviour in the scoping code. | Codex + Antigravity audit, adopted 2026-07-09 | `commit 9cda2f0; index.html:1370-1372` |
| ISO-4 | The cloud builds its 'only these stores' database query by pasting in the account's store list, so that list is now validated with a strict allowlist: only ordinary letters, numbers, dash and underscore survive. Anything else — a quote, a bracket, a space — means NO stores, never a partial query. | An auditor showed the store list could in principle be crafted to break out of the query and widen it. Testing showed SharePoint happened to reject those attempts today, but the fix does not rely on that luck. | Rules out the earlier reasoning that 'it is safe because the owner sets the store names'. | ISO-5 | When an account's store list changes, the device first pushes up anything it has not yet sent, then wipes out-of-scope data in one all-or-nothing operation, then re-downloads from scratch. If the wipe fails, a privacy lock is raised: backups are blocked, a warning shows, and it retries every cycle. The device never claims to be 'scoped and complete' until it really is. | Two separate dangers. Widening a scope would otherwise never deliver the new store's older records (the app only asks for records newer than the last one it saw). And a half-finished wipe would leave another store's data sitting on a franchise device while the app believed it was clean. | Rules out an ordinary top-up sync after a scope change. Rules out purging before unsent work has been flushed — that used to lose offline entries and is now blocked outright. | Codex + Antigravity audit, adopted; the flush-first fix was Kunal-approved org W3 work 2026-07-08 (decided) / fixed and extended 2026-07-13 in org wave 3 | `sync.js:2173-2233; AZURE-CHUNK10-SCOPE.md:105-109 and :118-122` |
